About this tracker

This tool keeps an eye on Microsoft's Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS), AKS Application Network, Azure Arc-enabled Kubernetes, AKS Arc, Kubernetes Fleet Manager, Azure Linux, Azure Container Registry (ACR), and Azure Application Gateway for Containers (AGC) documentation. AKS everywhere! It also shows the last 5 AKS and App Net release notes.

It automatically scans for changes, then uses AI to summarize and highlight updates that are most likely to matter — such as new features, deprecations, and significant content revisions.

Minor edits (like typos, formatting tweaks, and other low-impact changes) are usually filtered out. Because the process is automated, some updates may be missed or summaries may not capture every nuance.

For complete accuracy, you can always follow the provided links to the original Microsoft documentation.

With this tracker, you can:

  • Quickly scan meaningful AKS, AKS Automatic, AKS Support, App Net, Arc K8s, AKS Arc, Azure Linux, ACR, AGC, and Fleet documentation changes from the past 7 days
  • Stay up to date with the latest AKS and App Net release notes without digging through every doc page
  • Search and browse CVE security data across AKS releases and VHD node images, powered by the AKS Vulnerability Data API (Public Preview)

Get the Latest AKS Docs - Every Week, in Your Inbox

AKS Releases

Latest 5 AKS and App Net release notes with AI-generated summaries, breaking changes, and Good to Know information.

updated: 06/11/2026, 12:12:32

Release 2026-10-25

AKS
View Release
📅 2026-10-27

The release on 2026-10-25 introduces automatic upgrades for unsupported Kubernetes versions and updates to node pools.

❌

Breaking Changes

  • AKS will automatically upgrade unsupported clusters after a grace period.
  • Default OS SKU for node pools changes to Ubuntu 26.04 with Kubernetes 1.38.
🔑

Key Features

  • Kubernetes 1.37 is now generally available as Long-Term Support.
  • Node Auto Provisioning (NAP) enhancements include new CRDs and features.
💡

Good to Know

  • Review upgrade channels to prepare for automated upgrades.
  • Community support for Kubernetes 1.34 ends by November 2026.

Release Notes 2026-09-25

AKS
View Release
📅 2026-10-01

The 2026-09-25 release introduces significant updates and deprecations. Key features include the general availability of identity bindings and preview support for Ubuntu 26.04.

❌

Breaking Changes

  • Windows Server 2022 is not supported in Kubernetes version 1.37 and above.
  • Flatcar Container Linux node images are removed, affecting scaling operations.
  • The annotation service.beta.kubernetes.io/azure-pip-tags no longer overrides public IP tags.
🔑

Key Features

  • Identity bindings are now generally available.
  • Ubuntu 26.04 is available in preview.
  • Windows Server OS SKU migration to 2025 is in preview.
💡

Good to Know

  • AKS will auto-migrate deprecated Availability Sets clusters after September 30, 2026.
  • AKS release order has been updated for Central US and East US.
  • Check AKS Release Tracker for detailed release timing.

Release 2026-09-04

AKS
View Release
📅 2026-09-09

This release introduces Kubernetes 1.37 and autoscaling for VM node pools. It also announces the retirement of Azure Linux with OS Guard.

❌

Breaking Changes

  • AKS will remove Azure Linux with OS Guard node images on March 10, 2027.
  • AKS no longer produces VHDs for Windows Server 2019 and version 23H2.
🔑

Key Features

  • Autoscaling for Virtual Machines node pools is now generally available.
  • Kubernetes patch versions 1.36.3, 1.35.7, and 1.34.10 are available.
  • On-Demand Monitor for node health checks is in preview.
💡

Good to Know

  • LocalDNS is enabled automatically for compatible cluster networking.
  • AKS rejects updates that remove IPv6 from dual-stack clusters.
  • Managed namespace creation rejects names starting with reserved prefixes.

AppNet 2026-08-19

App Net
View Release
📅 2026-08-31

AppNet is now in public preview with several updates and bug fixes.

❌

Breaking Changes

  • AppNet 1.0, 1.1, and 1.2 versions retired.
  • Moving AppNet resources across resource groups is now blocked.
🔑

Key Features

  • AppNet available in more Azure regions.
  • AppNet 1.5 updated to Istio 1.30.3.
💡

Good to Know

  • Cluster prerequisites validated at member-join time.
  • Fixed issues with member updates and version upgrades.

Release 2026-08-07

AKS
View Release
📅 2026-08-11

This release introduces several enhancements including Node Auto Provisioning and automatic availability zone placement.

🔑

Key Features

  • Node Auto Provisioning now supports restricted public network access
  • Automatic availability zone placement is enabled globally
  • Control-plane only upgrades to AKS Long Term Support are allowed
  • Node pool rollback feature is now generally available
  • Encryption in Transit for Azure File CSI driver is now available

Meaningful updates to AKS, AKS Automatic, AKS Support, App Net, Arc K8s, AKS Arc, Azure Linux, ACR, AGC, and Fleet docs from the last 7 days.

                          @@ -2,7 +2,7 @@  AI inference workloads don't behave like traditional stateless HTTP applications. Requests are often model-specific, long-running, expensive to serve, and sensitive to runtime signals such as accelerator availability, queue depth, request priority, token budget, and model server capacity. -Application Gateway for Containers inference gateway supports these workloads by integrating with the Kubernetes [Gateway API Inference Extension](https://gateway-api-inference-extension.sigs.k8s.io/), which adds inference-aware resources to the Gateway API model. By using the inference gateway, you can expose self-hosted model servers through Application Gateway for Containers with model-aware and load-aware routing behavior.+Application Gateway for Containers inference gateway supports these workloads by integrating with the Kubernetes [Gateway API Inference Extension](https://gateway-api-inference-extension.sigs.k8s.io/), which adds inference-aware backends to the Gateway API model. By using the inference gateway, you can expose self-hosted model servers through Application Gateway for Containers with model-aware and load-aware routing behavior.  The inference gateway is purpose-built for serving large language models (LLMs) and other inference workloads. It routes requests based on model server signals rather than generic load balancing, which lowers time to first token (TTFT), reduces timeouts under load, and improves GPU efficiency. Built on the ingress capabilities of Application Gateway for Containers, the inference gateway also lets you pair AI workloads with capabilities like the web application firewall (WAF) to secure traffic before it reaches your model servers. @@ -12,24 +12,50 @@ Important  The Application Gateway for Containers inference gateway is currently in preview. See the [Supplemental Terms of Use for Microsoft Azure Previews](https://azure.microsoft.com/support/legal/preview-supplemental-terms/) for legal terms that apply to Azure features that are in beta, preview, or otherwise not yet released into general availability. -## What the Gateway API Inference Extension provides+## Building blocks of the inference gateway -The Gateway API Inference Extension turns a Gateway API implementation into an inference gateway by adding inference-specific backend and scheduling concepts.+An inference gateway combines three parts: a Gateway API implementation that handles ingress, an `InferencePool` backend defined by the Gateway API Inference Extension, and an endpoint picker that chooses a model server pod for each request. Application Gateway for Containers provides the first part and a managed body-based routing processor. You provide the `InferencePool` and the endpoint picker in your cluster. -The extension introduces these primary resources and components:+- **InferencePool**: A backend resource (`inference.networking.k8s.io/v1`) that represents a group of model server pods, the port they serve on, and a reference to the endpoint picker that selects a pod for each inference request.+- **Endpoint Picker (EPP)**: The role, defined by the Gateway API Inference Extension, of the component that selects an endpoint for each request. The gateway calls the EPP by using the endpoint picker protocol defined by the extension. The EPP returns the model server pod that should serve the request. EPP describes a contract, not a single product, and any implementation of the protocol can fill the role.+- **Body-based routing (BBR)**: A request processor that inspects an OpenAI-compatible request body, extracts the model name, and makes it available to the gateway as the `X-Gateway-Model-Name` header for model-aware routing. -- **InferencePool**: A backend resource that represents a group of model server pods and the endpoint picker used to select a pod for each inference request.-- **InferenceObjective**: A resource that represents request-serving objectives, such as priority, for requests that share an InferencePool.-- **Endpoint Picker (EPP)**: A customer-provided extension that runs in your cluster and implements inference scheduling. The EPP receives request metadata, scores candidate model server pods by using configurable plugins (for example, queue depth, KV cache utilization, and prefix-cache affinity), and returns the selected endpoint. Because endpoint selection runs in the EPP, the available routing behaviors depend on the scorer plugins that your EPP enables.-- **Body-based routing (BBR)**: A request processor that can inspect an OpenAI-compatible request body, extract the model name, and make it available to the gateway as the `X-Gateway-Model-Name` header for model-aware routing.+Application Gateway for Containers runs the BBR processor as a managed part of the gateway, so there's no separate body-based routing or payload-processing tier to deploy, scale, or patch. Because the inference gateway uses the Gateway API Inference Extension API, you configure it through standard Gateway API resources, and platform teams can use a Kubernetes-native API model for inference traffic instead of introducing a separate ingress configuration model. -Application Gateway for Containers runs the BBR processor as a managed part of the gateway, so there's no separate body-based routing tier to deploy, scale, or patch. It integrates with a customer-provided EPP to support request-time routing decisions. See [Configure multi-cluster connectivity](multi-cluster-connectivity) for topology selection and CLI examples.  ## How components interact -The control plane connects to the member cluster's Kubernetes API server, watches for service and configuration changes, and pushes xDS updates to ztunnel and waypoint proxies. When multiple members exist, each control plane also exchanges service information through managed messaging so that every member has a consistent view of all services across the mesh.+The control plane connects to the member cluster's Kubernetes API server, watches for service and configuration changes, and pushes xDS updates to ztunnel and waypoint proxies. When multiple members exist, Application Network synchronizes discovery for participating global services so the control planes can configure cross-cluster communication. -Certificate management provisions a root CA when the Application Network resource is created and issues intermediate CAs for each member cluster. Workload certificates are issued with a 24-hour validity period and rotated automatically every 12 hours. The entire certificate lifecycle, from root CA through intermediate and workload certificates, is managed without manual intervention.+Certificate management provisions a unique root CA when the Application Network resource is created and issues a dedicated intermediate CA for each Application Network member. Workload certificates are issued with a 24-hour validity period and rotated automatically every 12 hours. The entire certificate lifecycle, from root CA through intermediate and workload certificates, is managed without manual intervention.  For the full certificate hierarchy and rotation schedule, see the [Overview of Azure Kubernetes Application Network security](security).

                                  @@ -2,45 +2,323 @@  ## Summary -This article discusses how to identify and resolve the `K8SAPIServerConnFailVMExtensionError` error (also known as error code ERR\_K8S\_API\_SERVER\_CONN\_FAIL, error number 51) that occurs when you try to start or create and deploy a Microsoft Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS) cluster.+This article describes how to identify and resolve the `K8SAPIServerConnFailVMExtensionError` error (also known as error code `ERR_K8S_API_SERVER_CONN_FAIL`, error number 51). This error might occur when you create, start, scale, or upgrade a Microsoft Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS) cluster or node pool.++When AKS provisions a Linux node, the node bootstrap script (delivered through the `vmssCSE` Custom Script Extension) checks whether the node can reach the cluster's Kubernetes API server on TCP port 443 before it starts the kubelet. If the node can resolve the API server address but can't establish a connection to it, the script exits with code 51, the node isn't registered in the cluster, and the operation fails.++In most cases, this error is caused by a network configuration in the customer's environment (such as a firewall, network security group, route table, proxy, or authorized IP range) that blocks or drops traffic from the node subnet to the API server.++Note++- This article applies to Linux nodes. If the node can't *resolve* the API server name, you receive a different error. For more information, see [Troubleshoot the K8SAPIServerDNSLookupFailVMExtensionError error code (52)](error-code-k8sapiserverdnslookupfailvmextensionerror). If the node can't reach required outbound endpoints such as `mcr.microsoft.com`, see [Troubleshoot the OutboundConnFailVMExtensionError error code (50)](error-code-outboundconnfailvmextensionerror).+- If your cluster uses [API Server VNet Integration](/en-us/azure/aks/api-server-vnet-integration), a blocked connection to the API server usually causes a different error. See Clusters that use API Server VNet Integration.  ## Prerequisites -- The [Netcat](https://linuxcommandlibrary.com/man/netcat) (nc) command-line tool+- [Azure CLI](/en-us/cli/azure/install-azure-cli), signed in to the subscription that contains the AKS cluster.+- Permissions to read the AKS cluster and to run commands on the virtual machine scale sets (VMSS) or virtual machines (VMs) in the cluster's node resource group (for example, the [Virtual Machine Contributor](/en-us/azure/role-based-access-control/built-in-roles/compute#virtual-machine-contributor) role).++The connectivity tests in this article run on the AKS nodes by using [Azure Run Command](/en-us/azure/virtual-machines/linux/run-command). They use the [curl](https://curl.se/docs/manpage.html) command-line tool (or [Netcat](https://linuxcommandlibrary.com/man/netcat) (`nc`) for clusters that use API Server VNet Integration). These tools are already included in AKS Linux node images, so you don't have to install them on your local computer or on the nodes.  ## Symptoms -When you try to start or create an AKS cluster, you receive the following error message:-->-> Unable to establish connection from agents to Kubernetes API server, please see https://aka.ms/aks-required-ports-and-addresses for more information.->-> Details: Code="VMExtensionProvisioningError"->-> Message="VM has reported a failure when processing extension 'vmssCSE'.->-> Error message: "**Enable failed: failed to execute command: command terminated with exit status=51**\n[stdout]\n{->-> "ExitCode": "51",->-> "Output": "Thu Oct 14 18:07:37 UTC 2021,aks-nodepool1-18315663-vmss000000\nConnection to+When you try to create, start, scale, or upgrade an AKS cluster or node pool, the operation fails and you receive an error message that resembles the following example (identifiers are replaced with placeholders, and repeated progress output is removed):++```output+Code: K8SAPIServerConnFailVMExtensionError+Message: Create or update VMSS <vmss-resource-id> failed. Multiple VM extensions failed to be provisioned on the VM. The Extensions failed due to the errors:++[Extension Name: '<vmss-name>-AKSLinuxBilling'+Error Message: The handler for VM extension type 'Microsoft.AKS.Compute.AKS.Linux.Billing' has reported terminal failure for VM extension '<vmss-name>-AKSLinuxBilling' with error message: 'Dependent Extension Microsoft.Azure.Extensions.CustomScript did not succeed. Status was error'.]++[Extension Name: 'vmssCSE'+Error Message: VM has reported a user failure when processing extension 'vmssCSE'. Please correct the error and try again. (publisher 'Microsoft.Azure.Extensions' and type 'CustomScript'). Error code: '2'. Error message: 'failed to execute command: command terminated with exit status=51+[stdout]+{ "ExitCode": "51", "Output": "...+* connect to <api-server-ip> port 443 from <node-ip> port <port> failed: Connection timed out+* Failed to connect to <api-server-fqdn> port 443 after <n> ms: Couldn't connect to server+curl: (28) Failed to connect to <api-server-fqdn> port 443 after <n> ms: Couldn't connect to server+...+API server connection check code: 51++ exit 51", ... }+```++Key indicators of this error:++- The `vmssCSE` extension fails with `exit status=51` and the output contains `API server connection check code: 51`.+- The output shows a failed connection to the API server on port 443 (for example, `Connection timed out`, `Connection refused`, or `curl: (28) Failed to connect`).+- Other AKS extensions on the same VM (such as `AKSLinuxBilling`) might also fail with `Dependent Extension Microsoft.Azure.Extensions.CustomScript did not succeed`. This error is a side effect of the `vmssCSE` failure, not a separate problem.++In some API versions or tools, the message might begin with `Unable to establish connection from agents to Kubernetes API server, please see https://aka.ms/aks-required-ports-and-addresses for more information.` followed by `Code="VMExtensionProvisioningError"`.  ## Cause -Your cluster nodes can't connect to your cluster API server pod.+During node provisioning, the bootstrap script validates connectivity from the node to the API server before it starts the kubelet:++| Cluster configuration | Address that the node uses | Check performed by the bootstrap script |+| --- | --- | --- |+| Public cluster | Public API server FQDN (`<dns-prefix>-<hash>.hcp.<region>.azmk8s.io`) | DNS lookup, then `curl https://<api-server-fqdn>:443` (with retries) |+| Private cluster | Private API server FQDN (for example, `<dns-prefix>-<hash>.<guid>.privatelink.<region>.azmk8s.io`) that resolves to the private endpoint IP | DNS lookup, then `curl https://<api-server-fqdn>:443` (with retries) |++If the DNS lookup fails, the script exits with code 52 or 53. If the DNS lookup succeeds but the TCP/TLS connection to port 443 can't be established within the retry period, the script exits with code 51.++The node must be able to reach the API server for AKS to work. This requirement is documented in [Outbound network and FQDN rules for AKS clusters](/en-us/azure/aks/outbound-rules-control-egress#azure-global-required-fqdn--application-rules): `*.hcp.<location>.azmk8s.io` on HTTPS port 443 is required for node-to-API server communication.++Common reasons why the connection fails include:++- **Egress filtering blocks the API server.** A firewall, network virtual appliance (NVA), or proxy on the egress path doesn't allow TCP 443 to the API server FQDN (`*.hcp.<region>.azmk8s.io`) or to the API server public IP address.+- **TLS inspection or a proxy interferes with the connection.** A device that intercepts, decrypts, or rewrites TLS traffic (including the Application-Layer Protocol Negotiation (ALPN) extension) to the API server breaks the connection.+- **Network security group (NSG) rules deny the traffic.** An outbound rule on the node subnet or NIC blocks TCP 443 from the nodes to the API server.+- **User-defined routes (UDRs) send traffic to the wrong next hop.** A route table on the node subnet sends API server traffic to an NVA that drops it, routes it asymmetrically, or has no path to the destination.+- **API server authorized IP ranges don't include the cluster's egress IP.** If [authorized IP ranges](/en-us/azure/aks/api-server-authorized-ip-ranges) are enabled and the nodes egress through an IP address that isn't in the list (for example, the public IP of an Azure Firewall, NAT gateway, or other NVA), the API server rejects the connection.+- **(Private clusters) The private FQDN resolves to the wrong IP address.** Custom DNS servers or a manually managed private DNS zone return an outdated or incorrect IP address, so the node connects to an address where the API server isn't listening.+- **(Private clusters) The node can't reach the private endpoint.** For example, NSG rules or UDRs block traffic to the private endpoint IP, or the private endpoint is in a peered VNet that isn't reachable from the node subnet.  ## Solution -Run a Netcat command to verify that your nodes can resolve the cluster's fully qualified domain name (FQDN):+### Step 1: Gather cluster information++1. Get the API server endpoints and the network configuration of the cluster by running the [az aks show](/en-us/cli/azure/aks#az-aks-show) command:++    ```azurecli+    az aks show \+        --resource-group <resource-group> \+        --name <cluster-name> \+        --query "{fqdn:fqdn, privateFqdn:privateFqdn, nodeResourceGroup:nodeResourceGroup, outboundType:networkProfile.outboundType, privateCluster:apiServerAccessProfile.enablePrivateCluster, vnetIntegration:apiServerAccessProfile.enableVnetIntegration, authorizedIpRanges:apiServerAccessProfile.authorizedIpRanges}" \+        --output json+    ```++    Use the output to determine the API server FQDN that the nodes use:++    - **Public cluster**: Use the `fqdn` value.+    - **Private cluster**: Use the `privateFqdn` value. If `privateFqdn` is empty (for example, when the private DNS zone is set to `none`), use the `fqdn` value.++    If `vnetIntegration` is `true`, see Clusters that use API Server VNet Integration instead.++    The error message usually contains the API server FQDN that the node tried to reach (for example, `Failed to connect to <api-server-fqdn> port 443`).+2. Identify the node pool instances that failed provisioning.++    - For node pools that use **Virtual Machine Scale Sets** (default):++        ```azurecli+        az vmss list \+            --resource-group <node-resource-group> \+            --query "[].{name:name, nodePool:tags.\"aks-managed-poolName\", provisioningState:provisioningState}" \+            --output table++        az vmss list-instances \+            --resource-group <node-resource-group> \+            --name <vmss-name> \+            --query "[].{instanceId:instanceId, name:name, provisioningState:provisioningState}" \+            --output table+        ```+    - For [Virtual Machines node pools](/en-us/azure/aks/virtual-machines-node-pools):++        ```azurecli+        az vm list \+            --resource-group <node-resource-group> \+            --query "[?tags.\"aks-managed-poolName\"=='<node-pool-name>'].{name:name, provisioningState:provisioningState}" \+            --output table+        ```++### Step 2: Test connectivity from the node to the API server++Because the node didn't register in the cluster, you can't use `kubectl debug` to access it. Instead, use [Azure Run Command](/en-us/azure/virtual-machines/linux/run-command) to run the same check that the bootstrap script runs.++Note++- The VM or VMSS instance must still exist and be running. If AKS already removed the failed instance, run the tests from a test VM that you deploy in the same node subnet (with the same NSG and route table).+- Run Command returns only the last 4,096 bytes of output, and only one Run Command can run on a VM at a time.+- The `--query "value[0].message" --output tsv` parameters in the following commands print the command output as readable text instead of JSON.++The following command is the same check that the bootstrap script runs. It sends an HTTPS request to the API server FQDN by using the cluster certificate authority (`/etc/kubernetes/certs/ca.crt`) that's already on the node. The verbose (`-v`) output shows the IP address that the FQDN resolves to and where the connection fails, so a separate DNS lookup isn't required. The `--max-time 30` parameter stops the test after 30 seconds if the API server doesn't respond.++- For a **Virtual Machine Scale Sets** node pool, run the [az vmss run-command invoke](/en-us/cli/azure/vmss/run-command#az-vmss-run-command-invoke) command:++    ```azurecli+    az vmss run-command invoke \+        --resource-group <node-resource-group> \+        --name <vmss-name> \+        --instance-id <instance-id> \+        --command-id RunShellScript \+        --scripts "curl -v --max-time 30 --cacert /etc/kubernetes/certs/ca.crt https://<api-server-fqdn>:443" \+        --query "value[0].message" \+        --output tsv+    ```+- For a **Virtual Machines** node pool, run the [az vm run-command invoke](/en-us/cli/azure/vm/run-command#az-vm-run-command-invoke) command:++    ```azurecli+    az vm run-command invoke \+        --resource-group <node-resource-group> \+        --name <vm-name> \+        --command-id RunShellScript \+        --scripts "curl -v --max-time 30 --cacert /etc/kubernetes/certs/ca.crt https://<api-server-fqdn>:443" \+        --query "value[0].message" \+        --output tsv+    ```++If the node can reach the API server, the `[stdout]` section contains an `Unauthorized` response from the API server, and the `[stderr]` section shows a successful connection. The `401` response is expected because the request is anonymous. The following example is abbreviated: -```shell-nc -vz <cluster-fqdn> 443+```output+Enable succeeded:+[stdout]+{+  "kind": "Status",+  "apiVersion": "v1",+  "metadata": {},+  "status": "Failure",+  "message": "Unauthorized",+  "reason": "Unauthorized",+  "code": 401+}+[stderr]+* Host <api-server-fqdn>:443 was resolved.+* IPv4: <api-server-ip>+*   Trying <api-server-ip>:443...+* Connected to <api-server-fqdn> (<api-server-ip>) port 443+...+*  SSL certificate verify ok.+...+< HTTP/2 401+... ``` -If you're using egress filtering through a firewall, make sure that traffic is allowed to your cluster FQDN.+If the node can't reach the API server, the `[stdout]` section is empty and curl reports the error at the end of the `[stderr]` section. For example:++```output+Enable succeeded:+[stdout]++[stderr]+* Host <api-server-fqdn>:443 was resolved.+* IPv4: <api-server-ip>+*   Trying <api-server-ip>:443...+* Connection timed out after 30001 milliseconds+* Closing connection+curl: (28) Connection timed out after 30001 milliseconds+```++Note++`Enable succeeded` only means that Run Command ran the script. It doesn't mean that the connectivity test passed.++#### Review the node provisioning log (optional)++To see the exact command and output of the failed check, review the end of the node provisioning log:++```azurecli+az vmss run-command invoke \+    --resource-group <node-resource-group> \+    --name <vmss-name> \+    --instance-id <instance-id> \+    --command-id RunShellScript \+    --scripts "grep -E 'curl -v --cacert|API server connection check code' /var/log/azure/cluster-provision.log | tail -n 20" \+    --query "value[0].message" \+    --output tsv+```++For a Virtual Machines node pool, use `az vm run-command invoke --resource-group <node-resource-group> --name <vm-name>` with the same `--command-id`, `--scripts`, `--query`, and `--output` values.++### Step 3: Interpret the results++| Result | Meaning | Next step |+| --- | --- | --- |+| curl returns an HTTP response (for example, `HTTP/2 401` with an `Unauthorized` status) | The node can now reach the API server. The issue might have been transient or already fixed. | Retry the operation (see Step 5). |+| `curl: (6) Could not resolve host` | The node can't resolve the API server name. | See [Troubleshoot the K8SAPIServerDNSLookupFailVMExtensionError error code (52)](error-code-k8sapiserverdnslookupfailvmextensionerror). |+| The IP address in the `IPv4:` or `Trying` line isn't the expected API server IP address (private clusters) | DNS returns an incorrect or outdated record. | Verify that the private DNS zone record matches the private endpoint IP and that custom DNS servers forward the zone to Azure DNS (`168.63.129.16`). See [Private AKS clusters](/en-us/azure/aks/private-clusters). |+| `curl: (28)` (`Connection timed out` or `Operation timed out`) | Packets are dropped on the path (NSG, firewall, NVA, UDR, or authorized IP ranges). | Go to Step 4. |+| `curl: (7)` (`Connection refused`), `curl: (35)` or `curl: (56)` (`Connection reset by peer`) | A device on the path actively rejects or resets the connection, or the node connects to the wrong IP address. | Check firewalls, proxies, and NVAs on the path, and verify the resolved IP address. |+| A TLS error, such as `curl: (60) SSL certificate problem` or `curl: (35) ... unexpected eof while reading` | A proxy or TLS inspection device intercepts the connection. | Exclude the API server FQDN from TLS inspection, and don't modify the ALPN extension. |++### Step 4: Fix the network configuration++Based on the cluster configuration, verify the following items.++Tip++To check Azure networking resources (NSGs, route tables, Azure Firewall, NAT gateway, load balancer, and VNet peering) along the path from a node pool to a destination, you can also run a connectivity analysis by using [Azure Virtual Network Verifier](/en-us/azure/virtual-network-manager/concept-virtual-network-verifier). This tool doesn't evaluate third-party or on-premises devices.++#### Public clusters++- If you restrict egress traffic through a firewall or NVA, allow TCP 443 to `*.hcp.<region>.azmk8s.io` (application rule) or to the API server public IP address (network rule). For the complete list of requirements, see [Outbound network and FQDN rules for AKS clusters](/en-us/azure/aks/outbound-rules-control-egress) and [Limit network traffic with Azure Firewall in AKS](/en-us/azure/aks/limit-egress-traffic).+- Make sure that NSGs on the node subnet and node NICs don't deny outbound TCP 443 to the API server.+- Make sure that TLS inspection and proxies don't intercept or alter traffic to the API server FQDN.+- If API server authorized IP ranges are enabled, make sure that the public IP address that the nodes use for egress (for example, the Azure Firewall, NAT gateway, or NVA public IP) is included. For more information, see [Secure access to the API server using authorized IP address ranges in AKS](/en-us/azure/aks/api-server-authorized-ip-ranges).++#### Private clusters++- Verify that the API server FQDN resolves to the private endpoint IP address from the node subnet. If you use custom DNS servers, make sure that they forward the private DNS zone to Azure DNS (`168.63.129.16`) or otherwise return the correct record.+- Make sure that NSGs and UDRs on the node subnet allow traffic to the private endpoint IP address on TCP 443, and that the traffic isn't routed through an NVA that drops it.+- For more information, see [Create a private AKS cluster](/en-us/azure/aks/private-clusters).++### Step 5: Retry the operation++After you fix the network configuration, run the connectivity test from Step 2 again to confirm that the node can reach the API server. Then, retry the operation that failed (create, start, scale, or upgrade). If the cluster or node pool remains in a failed state, you can reconcile the cluster by running the [az aks update](/en-us/cli/azure/aks#az-aks-update) command without other parameters:++```azurecli+az aks update --resource-group <resource-group> --name <cluster-name>+```++## Clusters that use API Server VNet Integration++In clusters that use [API Server VNet Integration](/en-us/azure/aks/api-server-vnet-integration), the nodes connect directly to the private IP address of the API server internal load balancer in the delegated API server subnet, without a DNS lookup. The bootstrap script tests this connection by using `nc -vz <api-server-ip> 443` instead of curl, and it retries the test for longer than the time limit of the Custom Script Extension.++Because of this, if the connection is blocked, node provisioning usually fails with a Custom Script Extension timeout instead of the `K8SAPIServerConnFailVMExtensionError` error (exit code 51). For example, the error contains `CSETimeoutVMExtensionError`, `exit status=124`, `nc -vz <api-server-ip> 443`, and `CSE timeout approaching, exiting early`.++To check whether the connection to the API server is blocked, follow these steps:++1. Get the private IP address of the API server. The IP address also appears in the error message (`nc -vz <api-server-ip> 443`).++    ```azurecli+    az network lb show \+        --resource-group <node-resource-group> \+        --name kube-apiserver \+        --query "frontendIPConfigurations[].privateIPAddress" \+        --output tsv+    ```+2. Test the connection from an affected node by running the same Netcat check that the bootstrap script runs. Use the [az vmss run-command invoke](/en-us/cli/azure/vmss/run-command#az-vmss-run-command-invoke) command for a Virtual Machine Scale Sets node pool, or the [az vm run-command invoke](/en-us/cli/azure/vm/run-command#az-vm-run-command-invoke) command for a Virtual Machines node pool:++    ```azurecli+    az vmss run-command invoke \+        --resource-group <node-resource-group> \+        --name <vmss-name> \+        --instance-id <instance-id> \+        --command-id RunShellScript \+        --scripts "nc -vz -w 10 <api-server-ip> 443" \+        --query "value[0].message" \+        --output tsv+    ```++    ```azurecli+    az vm run-command invoke \+        --resource-group <node-resource-group> \+        --name <vm-name> \+        --command-id RunShellScript \+        --scripts "nc -vz -w 10 <api-server-ip> 443" \+        --query "value[0].message" \+        --output tsv+    ```++    Netcat writes its result to the `[stderr]` section. A successful test shows `Connection to <api-server-ip> 443 port [tcp/https] succeeded!`. A failed test shows an error such as `nc: connect to <api-server-ip> port 443 (tcp) timed out` or `nc: connect to <api-server-ip> port 443 (tcp) failed: Connection refused`.+3. If the test fails, check what blocks the traffic between the node subnet and the API server subnet:++    - Make sure that NSG rules on the node subnet, the node NICs, and the API server subnet allow the following communication:++        | Destination | Source | Protocol | Port |+        | --- | --- | --- | --- |+        | API server subnet CIDR | Cluster (node) subnet | TCP | 443 and 4443 |+        | API server subnet CIDR | Azure Load Balancer | TCP | 9988 |+    - Make sure that UDRs on the node subnet don't send traffic for the API server subnet through an NVA or firewall that drops it or routes it asymmetrically. -In rare cases, the firewall's outbound IP address can be blocked if you've authorized IP addresses that are enabled on your cluster. In this scenario, you must add the outbound IP address of your firewall to the list of authorized IP ranges for the cluster. For more information, see [Secure access to the API server using authorized IP address ranges in AKS](/en-us/azure/aks/api-server-authorized-ip-ranges).+    For more information, see [API Server VNet Integration - NSG security rules](/en-us/azure/aks/api-server-vnet-integration#network-security-group-nsg-security-rules).+4. After you fix the network configuration, run the Netcat test again, and then retry the operation.  ## More information  - [General troubleshooting of AKS cluster creation issues](troubleshoot-aks-cluster-creation-issues)+- [Troubleshoot the OutboundConnFailVMExtensionError error code (50)](error-code-outboundconnfailvmextensionerror)+- [Troubleshoot the K8SAPIServerDNSLookupFailVMExtensionError error code (52)](error-code-k8sapiserverdnslookupfailvmextensionerror)+- [Outbound network and FQDN rules for AKS clusters](/en-us/azure/aks/outbound-rules-control-egress)+- [API Server VNet Integration in AKS](/en-us/azure/aks/api-server-vnet-integration)+- [Create a private AKS cluster](/en-us/azure/aks/private-clusters)+- [Run scripts in a Linux VM by using action Run Commands](/en-us/azure/virtual-machines/linux/run-command)

When an AKS cluster joins the Application Network as a member, Application Network issues an **intermediate certificate** for that member signed by the Application Network root CA. The member's intermediate CA issues short-lived **workload certificates** to workloads in its connected AKS cluster.  All workload certificates inherit trust from the Application Network root CA, maintaining a unified trust boundary across the resource. The rotation of root and intermediate certificates is handled transparently for users without causing any downtime or traffic disruptions. Loki is the log aggregation backend.++### Solution: Configure Grafana Alloy to collect logs directly from nodes++To prevent excessive traffic through the Kubernetes API server and Konnectivity tunnel, configure Grafana Alloy to collect container logs directly from the AKS nodes.++Use node-level log collection instead of Kubernetes API-based log streaming.++With this configuration:++1. Grafana Alloy runs on the cluster nodes, typically as a DaemonSet.+2. Alloy reads container logs directly from the node filesystem.+3. Collected logs are forwarded to the configured Loki endpoint.+4. Log collection no longer relies on the API server and Konnectivity tunnel for retrieving container log contents.++This approach reduces API server and Konnectivity tunnel traffic and helps prevent tunnel saturation caused by high-volume log collection.++For configuration guidance, see the Grafana documentation:++- [Grafana Alloy - Collect Kubernetes logs](https://grafana.com/docs/alloy/latest/collect/logs-in-kubernetes/)+- [Grafana Alloy - loki.source.file](https://grafana.com/docs/alloy/latest/reference/components/loki/loki.source.file/)+ **Third-party contact disclaimer**  Microsoft provides third-party contact information to help you find additional information about this topic. This contact information may change without notice. Microsoft does not guarantee the accuracy of third-party contact information.

The sample uses one head pod and one worker pod.++Set the sample resource names and Ray image:++```bash+RAY_NAMESPACE="kuberay-sample"+RAY_CLUSTER_NAME="sample-raycluster"+RAY_JOB_NAME="sample-rayjob"+RAY_VERSION="2.58.0"+RAY_IMAGE="mcr.microsoft.com/aks/ai-runtime/ray@sha256:e9a329eef15e5a13d50c3186c2b8036cd3728c6e218da860de23e8494b99ddd3"+```++Create a namespace and deploy the `RayCluster`:++```bash+kubectl create namespace "$RAY_NAMESPACE"++kubectl apply -f - <<YAML+apiVersion: ray.io/v1+kind: RayCluster+metadata:+  name: ${RAY_CLUSTER_NAME}+  namespace: ${RAY_NAMESPACE}+spec:+  rayVersion: "${RAY_VERSION}"+  headGroupSpec:+    rayStartParams:+      num-cpus: "0"+      dashboard-host: "0.0.0.0"+    template:+      spec:+        containers:+        - name: ray-head+          image: ${RAY_IMAGE}+          resources:+            requests:+              cpu: 500m+              memory: 2Gi+            limits:+              cpu: "1"+              memory: 4Gi+  workerGroupSpecs:+  - groupName: cpu-workers+    replicas: 1+    minReplicas: 1+    maxReplicas: 1+    rayStartParams:+      num-cpus: "1"+    template:+      spec:+        containers:+        - name: ray-worker+          image: ${RAY_IMAGE}+          resources:+            requests:+              cpu: 500m+              memory: 1Gi+            limits:+              cpu: "1"+              memory: 2Gi+YAML+```++Wait for the `RayCluster` to report `ready`, and then confirm that the head and worker pods are running:++```bash+kubectl wait \+  --for=jsonpath='{.status.state}'=ready \+  raycluster/"$RAY_CLUSTER_NAME" \+  --namespace "$RAY_NAMESPACE" \+  --timeout=10m++kubectl get raycluster,pods --namespace "$RAY_NAMESPACE"+```++Submit an HTTP-mode `RayJob` to the existing cluster. resource group lockdown | `ReadOnly` | [Node resource group lockdown](../node-resource-group-lockdown) |++### Plan your migration++1. Determine whether your cluster uses an AKS-managed virtual network or a custom virtual network. If the following command returns a virtual network name, your cluster uses an AKS-managed virtual network. If it returns nothing, your cluster uses a custom virtual network.++    ```azurecli+    az network vnet list --resource-group "${NODE_RESOURCE_GROUP}" --query "[].name" --output tsv+    ```++    Several steps in this article differ depending on your virtual network type.+2. Check your cluster's current network plugin, outbound type, load balancer SKU, and node resource group restriction level:++    ```azurecli+    az aks show \+        --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+        --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+        --query "{networkPlugin:networkProfile.networkPlugin, networkPluginMode:networkProfile.networkPluginMode, outboundType:networkProfile.outboundType, loadBalancerSku:networkProfile.loadBalancerSku, nrgRestrictionLevel:nodeResourceGroupProfile.restrictionLevel}" \+        --output table+    ```++    - **Network plugin**: Must be `azure` with plugin mode `overlay`. If your cluster uses kubenet or Azure CNI without Overlay, update it to Azure CNI Overlay before you continue. For more information, see [Update the IPAM mode to Azure CNI Overlay](../update-azure-cni#update-the-ipam-mode-to-azure-cni-overlay).+    - **Load balancer SKU**: Must be `standard`. If your cluster still uses `basic`, update it with `az aks update --load-balancer-sku standard`.+    - **Node resource group restriction level**: For an AKS-managed virtual network, you need to create a subnet in the node resource group. If the restriction level is `ReadOnly`, set it to `Unrestricted` before you continue. You lock it down again in a later step.+3. Review the changes that disrupt running workloads or access, and plan a maintenance window:++    - Changing the outbound type changes your cluster's egress IP addresses. Update any firewall rules that allow traffic from the cluster.+    - Enabling API Server VNet Integration changes the API server IP address and requires a cluster restart. This change is permanent.+    - Changing the network dataplane, OS SKU, and SSH access settings reimages your nodes.+    - Disabling local accounts removes static kubeconfig credentials. Ensure you have access through Microsoft Entra ID and an Azure RBAC role, such as *Azure Kubernetes Service RBAC Cluster Admin*, before you disable them.+    - The migration enables Deployment Safeguards in `Enforce` mode with baseline Pod Security Standards. Deployments that don't comply are rejected. You can exclude namespaces, but you can't switch the cluster-wide level to `Warn`. Review your workloads before you migrate. For more information, see [Use Deployment Safeguards](../deployment-safeguards#excluding-namespaces).++### Prepare cluster networking++1. **Outbound type**: For an AKS-managed virtual network, the outbound type must be a managed NAT gateway. The `loadBalancer` outbound type isn't supported. If needed, update the outbound type:++    ```azurecli+    az aks update \+        --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+        --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+        --outbound-type managedNATGateway \+        --outbound-type-sku StandardV2+    ```++    For a custom virtual network, any outbound type is supported and no change is required. For more information, see [Update outboundType after cluster creation](../egress-outboundtype#update-outboundtype-after-cluster-creation).+2. **Cluster identity**: For an AKS-managed virtual network, keep the cluster's system-assigned managed identity. For a custom virtual network, the cluster must use a user-assigned managed identity that has the *Network Contributor* role on the virtual network. If needed, assign the identity:++    ```azurecli+    az aks update \+        --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+        --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+        --enable-managed-identity \+        --assign-identity "${IDENTITY_RESOURCE_ID}" \+        --yes+    ```++    Replace `IDENTITY_RESOURCE_ID` with the resource ID of your user-assigned managed identity. For more information, see [Use a user-assigned managed identity](../user-assigned-managed-identity).+3. **API server subnet**: Create a dedicated subnet for the API server in the same virtual network as your nodes. The subnet must be separate from your node subnet, be at least `/28`, and be delegated to `Microsoft.ContainerService/managedClusters`.++    For an AKS-managed virtual network, create the subnet in the virtual network in the node resource group:++    ```azurecli+    export VNET_NAME=$(az network vnet list --resource-group "${NODE_RESOURCE_GROUP}" --query "[0].name" --output tsv)++    export APISERVER_SUBNET_ID=$(az network vnet subnet create \+        --resource-group "${NODE_RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+        --vnet-name "${VNET_NAME}" \+        --name apiserver-subnet \+        --address-prefixes "<unused-address-range>" \+        --delegations Microsoft.ContainerService/managedClusters \+        --query id --output tsv)+    ```++    For a custom virtual network, create the subnet in your own virtual network instead, and set `APISERVER_SUBNET_ID` to its resource ID.+4. **API Server VNet Integration**: Enable API Server VNet Integration with the subnet you created, and then restart the cluster:++    ```azurecli+    az aks update \+        --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+        --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+        --enable-apiserver-vnet-integration \+        --apiserver-subnet-id "${APISERVER_SUBNET_ID}"++    az aks stop --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}"+    az aks start --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}"+    ```++    For more information, see [Convert an existing AKS Standard cluster to API Server VNet Integration](../api-server-vnet-integration#convert-an-existing-aks-standard-cluster-to-api-server-vnet-integration).+5. **Network dataplane**: Update the network dataplane to Cilium. This command also sets the network policy to Cilium.++    ```azurecli+    az aks update \+        --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+        --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+        --network-dataplane cilium+    ```++    For more information, see [Azure CNI Powered by Cilium](../azure-cni-powered-by-cilium).++Important++Update the network dataplane before you enable Auto node provisioning mode in the next section. After Auto mode is enabled, you can't change the network dataplane.++### Prepare the system node pool and node provisioning++1. **Ephemeral OS disk**: The system node pool must use an ephemeral OS disk. Check the current OS disk type:++    ```azurecli+    az aks nodepool list \+        --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+        --cluster-name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+        --query "[?mode=='System'].{name:name, osDiskType:osDiskType}" \+        --output table+    ```++    You can't change the OS disk type of an existing node pool. If your system node pool uses a `Managed` OS disk, [add a new system node pool](../create-node-pools) with `--mode System --node-osdisk-type Ephemeral --os-sku AzureLinux --ssh-access disabled`, move your system workloads to it, and then [delete the old node pool](../delete-node-pool). You can then skip the next step.+2. **OS SKU and SSH access**: Update the system node pool to use Azure Linux and disable SSH access:++    ```azurecli+    az aks nodepool update \+        --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+        --cluster-name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+        --name "<system-node-pool-name>" \+        --os-sku AzureLinux \+        --ssh-access disabled \+        --yes+    ```++    This command reimages the node pool. Wait for it to finish before you make other changes to the node pool. For more information, see [Use the Azure Linux Container Host](../use-azure-linux) and [Manage SSH access on AKS cluster nodes](../manage-ssh-node-access).+3. **Node provisioning mode**: Enable Auto node provisioning mode:++    ```azurecli+    az aks update \+        --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+        --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+        --node-provisioning-mode Auto+    ```++    After you enable Auto mode, you can't revert to Manual mode without first removing all autoprovisioned nodes. For more information, see [Node auto-provisioning](../node-auto-provisioning).++### Enable required features and add-ons++1. Update the cluster tier to Standard, and enable the Azure Policy and Azure Key Vault Secrets Provider add-ons with secret rotation:++    ```azurecli+    az aks update --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" --tier standard++    az aks enable-addons \+        --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+        --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+        --addons azure-policy,azure-keyvault-secrets-provider \+        --enable-secret-rotation+    ```+2. Enable the remaining required features:++    ```azurecli+    az aks update \+        --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+        --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+        --auto-upgrade-channel stable \+        --node-os-upgrade-channel NodeImage \+        --enable-workload-identity \+        --enable-oidc-issuer \+        --enable-aad \+        --enable-azure-rbac \+        --disable-local-accounts \+        --enable-image-cleaner \+        --enable-vpa \+        --enable-keda \+        --yes+    ```++### Lock down the node resource group++Set the node resource group restriction level to `ReadOnly`. Do this step last, because lockdown blocks changes to resources in the node resource group, including the API server subnet you created earlier.++```azurecli+az aks update \+    --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+    --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+    --nrg-lockdown-restriction-level ReadOnly+```++For more information, see [Node resource group lockdown](../node-resource-group-lockdown).++### Migrate the cluster to AKS Automatic++If any requirement is still unmet, the migration command fails and lists the unmet requirements it detected. Some checks run only after earlier ones pass, so resolve the listed items and run the command again until it succeeds.++#### AKS-managed virtual network++```azurecli+az aks update \+    --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+    --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+    --sku automatic \+    --enable-hosted-system+```++#### Custom virtual network++Provide a subnet for the managed system node pool by using the `--system-node-subnet-id` parameter. For subnet requirements, see [Migrate an existing AKS Automatic cluster to managed system node pools](aks-automatic-managed-system-node-pools-about#migrate-an-existing-aks-automatic-cluster-to-managed-system-node-pools-preview). The `--node-subnet-id` parameter is optional. If you don't provide it, AKS uses the existing system node pool subnet.++```azurecli+az aks update \+    --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+    --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+    --sku automatic \+    --enable-hosted-system \+    --system-node-subnet-id "${SYSTEM_NODE_SUBNET_ID}" \+    --node-subnet-id "${NODE_SUBNET_ID}"+```++### Verify the migration++1. Confirm that the cluster uses the `automatic` SKU and `Standard` tier, and that the update finished successfully:++    ```azurecli+    az aks show \+        --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+        --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+        --query "{sku:sku, provisioningState:provisioningState}" \+        --output json+    ```++    Confirm `sku.name` is `Automatic`, `sku.tier` is `Standard`, and `provisioningState` is `Succeeded`.+2. Confirm the cluster uses managed system node pools:++    ```azurecli+    az aks show \+        --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" \+        --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}" \+        --query hostedSystemProfile \+        --output json+    ```++    Confirm `hostedSystemProfile.enabled` is `true`. For a custom virtual network, `systemNodeSubnetId` and `nodeSubnetId` show your subnet resource IDs. For an AKS-managed virtual network, these values are `null`.+3. Get credentials for the cluster and confirm all nodes are in the `Ready` state:++    ```azurecli+    az aks get-credentials --resource-group "${RESOURCE_GROUP}" --name "${RESOURCE_NAME}"+    kubectl get nodes -o wide+    ```++### Failure behavior++If migration can't provision managed system pool infrastructure (for example, because of regional capacity constraints), AKS preserves existing system and user node pools in their pre-migration state.

Higher `priority` values are served before lower ones when the EPP must shed load.+Create an `InferenceModelRewrite` when you want clients to request a stable model name while the EPP decides which model the pool actually serves. The EPP rewrites the `model` field before it sends the request to the model server, and returns the client's requested name in the response. Use this pattern to roll out a new model version or LoRA adapter on the same pool without changing clients. -Warning--The `InferenceObjective` resource is currently in the `inference.networking.x-k8s.io/v1alpha2` API group, while `InferencePool` has graduated to `v1`; both groups coexist while the `InferenceObjective` API stabilizes. Expect API changes to the resource.+The following example lets clients request `chat-default`, which the EPP maps to `${MODEL_NAME}`.  ```bash kubectl apply -n $NAMESPACE -f - <<EOF-apiVersion: inference.networking.x-k8s.io/v1alpha2-kind: InferenceObjective+apiVersion: llm-d.ai/v1alpha2+kind: InferenceModelRewrite metadata:-  name: critical-inference+  name: chat-default spec:   poolRef:     group: inference.networking.k8s.io     kind: InferencePool     name: ${INFERENCE_POOL_NAME}+  rules:+  - matches:+    - model:+        type: Exact+        value: chat-default+    targets:+    - modelRewrite: ${MODEL_NAME}+EOF+```++Send a request for `chat-default`.++```bash+curl -s http://${GATEWAY_ADDRESS}/v1/chat/completions \+  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \+  -d '{+    "model": "chat-default",+    "messages": [{"role": "user", "content": "What is 2+2? Answer in one word."}],+    "max_tokens": 10+  }'+```++The response comes from the vLLM server, and its `model` field is `chat-default`.++Note++Application Gateway for Containers matches the `HTTPRoute` before the EPP rewrites the model name, so `X-Gateway-Model-Name` carries the name that the client requested. If your route matches on `X-Gateway-Model-Name`, add a match for each client-facing name, such as `chat-default`, that sends requests to this `InferencePool`. A route that matches on path alone, like the one in this article, needs no change.++To split traffic across models that the pool serves, such as two LoRA adapters, list more than one target with weights. For example, the following targets send 90 percent of `chat-default` requests to `adapter-v1` and 10 percent to `adapter-v2`:++```yaml+    targets:+    - modelRewrite: adapter-v1+      weight: 90+    - modelRewrite: adapter-v2+      weight: 10+```++Note++If the pool uses `FailOpen` and the EPP is unavailable, requests bypass the EPP and reach the model server with the original model name, such as `chat-default`, which the model server doesn't recognize. Use `FailClose` for pools that rely on model rewrites.++## Optional: prioritize traffic with InferenceObjective++Create `InferenceObjective` resources when different classes of traffic share a pool and you want latency-critical requests served first when the pool is saturated. Clients select an objective with the `x-llm-d-inference-objective` request header. The `HTTPRoute` doesn't reference objectives.++Priority takes effect only when flow control is enabled in the llm-d router. With flow control, the EPP holds requests in priority bands while the pool is saturated, dispatches higher-priority requests first, and sheds lower-priority requests when their band is full or their time-to-live expires. Flow control is a feature gate and is off by default.++### Enable flow control++Create a second values file that replaces the EPP's scheduling configuration with one that enables flow control.++```bash+cat > llm-d-router-flow-control.yaml <<'EOF'+router:+  epp:+    pluginsConfigFile: flow-control.yaml+    pluginsCustomConfig:+      flow-control.yaml: |+        apiVersion: llm-d.ai/v1+        kind: EndpointPickerConfig+        featureGates:+        - flowControl+        plugins:+        - type: prefix-cache-scorer+        - type: queue-scorer+        - type: kv-cache-utilization-scorer+        - type: active-request-scorer+        # Treats a pod as saturated when it has maxConcurrency requests in flight.+        # Set it to the number of concurrent requests you want each replica to run.+        -
